## Neighborhood:

The subject property is located in Portland, Oregon, a city in Multnomah County. Portland is part of the Portland-Vancouver MSA, the largest city in Oregon. The subject is located in a mixed-use area located along W. Burnside Street, which is the divider between SW Portland (downtown/Goose Hollow) and NW Portland. Providence Park, home to two professional soccer teams, is located across W. Burnside street and 1 block to the south. The long-term outlook for the neighborhood is somewhat average with marginally stable property values anticipated coinciding with the economic progress of the region and the specific location.

## MARKET VALUE

This definition is in compliance with the OCC (Office of the Comptroller of the Currency), FDIC (Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation), 12 CFR part 34.42 FIRREA (Federal Institutions Reforms, Recovery, and Enforcement Act), and USPAP (Uniform Standards of Professional Appraisal Practice) as adopted by the Appraisal Foundation and the Appraisal Institute. This definition complies with the USPAP Regulations.¹

Market value is the major focus of most real property appraisal assignments. Both economic and legal definitions of market value have been developed and refined. A current economic definition agreed upon by federal financial institutions of the United States of America is:

The most probable price which a property should bring in a competitive and open market under all conditions requisite to a fair sale, the buyer and seller each acting prudently and knowledgeably, and assuming the price is not affected by undue stimulus. Implicit in this definition is the consummation of a Sale as of a specified date and the passing of title from seller to buyer under conditions whereby:

1) Buyer and seller are typically motivated;
2) Both parties are well informed or well advised, and acting in what they consider their best interests;
3) A reasonable time is allowed for exposure in the open market;
4) Payment is made in terms of cash in United States dollars or in terms of financial arrangements comparable thereto; and
5) The price represents the normal consideration for the property sold unaffected by special or creative financing or Sale concessions granted by anyone associated with the sale.

Substitution of another currency for United States dollars in the fourth condition is appropriate in countries or in reports addressed to clients from other countries.²

## DEFINITIONS³

### As Is Value

The value of specific ownership rights to an identified parcel of real estate as of the effective date of the appraisal; relates to what physically exists and is legally permissible and excludes all assumptions concerning hypothetical market conditions or possible rezoning.

### Hypothetical Condition

That which is contrary to what exists but is supposed for the purpose of analysis. Hypothetical conditions assume conditions contrary to known facts about physical, legal, or economic characteristics of the subject property, or about conditions external to the property such as market conditions or trends, or about the integrity of data used in an analysis. A hypothetical condition may be used in an assignment only if:

- Use of the hypothetical condition is clearly required for legal purposes, for purposes of reasonable analysis, or for purposes of comparison;
- Use of the hypothetical condition results in a credible analysis; and
- The appraiser complies with the disclosure requirements set forth in USPAP for hypothetical conditions.

### Prospective Value

A forecast of the value expected at a specified future date. A prospective value estimate is most frequently sought in connection with real estate projects that are proposed, under construction, or under conversion to a new use, or those that have not achieved sellout or a stabilized level of long-term occupancy at the time the appraisal report is written.

### Stabilized Value

A value estimate that excludes from consideration any transitory condition, e.g., a bonus or premium for material, the abnormal inefficiency of labor, the cost of delay, or unfinished repairs.

### Fee Simple Estate

Absolute ownership unencumbered by any other interest or estate, subject only to the limitations imposed by the governmental powers of taxation, eminent domain, police power, and escheat.

### Leased Fee Estate

An ownership interest held by a landlord with the rights of use and occupancy conveyed by lease to others. The rights of lessor (leased fee owner) and the leased fee are specified by contract terms contained within the lease.

### Leasehold Estate

An ownership interest held by the lessee (tenant or renter) through a lease conveying the rights of use and occupancy for a stated term under certain conditions.

# SUBJECT HISTORY OF OWNERSHIP INFORMATION

A preliminary title report was not provided. Based on county records, title to the property is vested in Cambridge-Trinity, LLC, which has owned and operated the subject since 2005. There are no other known transfers for the past 3 years.

|  Ownership (Past 3+ Years)  |   |   |   |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  Date of Sale/Ownership | Current Owner/Seller | Previous/Pending Owner | Conveyance Price  |
|  April 19, 2005 | Cambridge-Trinity, LLC | JB Equities, LLC & SJ Investments, LLC | $4,700,000  |

Subject Listing History: The overall portfolio, consisting of the subject property and the adjacent 30-unit Trinity Plaza, is currently listed for $6,450,000, reflecting an asking rate of $120.94 per square foot. According to Greg Frick of HFORE, the listing broker, the portfolio was initially brought to market several years ago at $8,900,000 and received multiple offers ranging from $5,000,000 to $6,000,000. The price has been reduced several times, with the most recent adjustment to the current list price occurring several months ago. No allocation was provided for the subject property alone; however, applying the asking rate of $120.94 per square foot to the subject's gross leasable area results in an illustrative value indication of approximately $4,380,000, which is slightly below the original purchase price. This dip is reflective of the decline of retail viability in the subject's micro market along W. Burnside Street.

The subject has remained on the market for an extended period, influenced by broader challenges in the downtown market, including civil unrest and an increasing homeless population. These conditions have negatively affected investor sentiment and tenant demand, particularly for retail space, though gradual improvement has been reported. The apartment component is comparatively better positioned, with ongoing unit updates and market-level rents, though it reflects an 8.16% vacancy rate (two rent-ready units and two down units). The retail portion contains two long-term vacancies totaling 4,082 square feet, representing 62.35% of the retail area.

Given the prolonged marketing exposure exceeding two years and the absence of a pending or closed transaction, the current list price appears above market expectations. The significant retail vacancy and the lower achievable lease rates for the vacant suites further suggest that additional price reductions may be necessary to attract investors. As such, this listing provides a limited and less reliable value indicator for the subject property, and the allocation derived from the asking price should be considered illustrative only.

# SCOPE OF THE APPRAISAL

The scope of this appraisal is to estimate the Prospective Leased Fee Value at Stabilized Occupancy and the As Is Leased Fee Value for the subject property. These steps include the following:

- Definition of pertinent issues related to the appraisal assignment
- Inspection of the subject property
- Site and improvement analyses
- Highest and best use analysis (as vacant, and as improved)
- Market data selection, verification, and analysis
- Use of the approaches to value and valuation
- The reconciliation of value indications and final value estimate

# VALUATION METHODOLOGY

The Income and Sales Comparison Approaches are performed and reconciled to develop our opinion of the Prospective Leased Fee Value at Stabilized Occupancy. Deductions for absorption (includes income loss from rent increases and deferred maintenance) are made to reconcile the As Is Leased Fee Value of the subject property. The Cost Approach was not presented in this appraisal due to the subjectivity and difficulty in determining accrued depreciation for older buildings. A typical purchaser of the subject property would not consider this approach as a reliable indicator. Therefore, the exclusion of this valuation approach is reasonable and does not reduce the credibility of this appraisal.

## MARKETING AND EXPOSURE TIME

Marketing time is the period of time it takes an interest in real property to sell on the market subsequent to the date of an appraisal. Exposure time is the estimated length of time the property interest being appraised would have been on the market prior to the hypothetical consummation of a sale at market value on the effective date of appraisal. The market and exposure periods are deemed to be equivalent. Discussions with brokers active in the market indicate that there is stable demand for well-located, functional properties in the immediate subject area. The sale comparables presented below are noted along with the anticipated marketing and exposure period.

|  Property Type | Comparables- Marketing and Exposure | Forecast- Market and Exposure  |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Mixed-Use Developments | Unknown/137 Days to 2 Years | 365 Days or Less  |

The subject (plus Trinity Plaza) has been on the market for over 2 years, initially priced at $8,900,000. Offers ranged between $5,000,000 and $6,000,000 at the time. The price has dropped consistently to a current list price of $6,450,000. If the price isn't dropped, the extended marketing period will increase. Overall, this is not a good indicator for the subject, with the current allocated list price ($4,380,000) being 11.73% higher than the concluded As-Is Leased Fee Value. Based on the subject's physical and location characteristics, the subject's marketing time and the comparables, marketing and exposure periods are noted above. This assumes that the subject property was competently promoted and appropriately priced.

## Page 17

MARKET AREA ANALYSIS

The Appraisal Institute defines market area as following: "The geographic or locational delineation of the market for a specific category of real estate, i.e., the area in which alternative, similar properties effectively compete with the subject property in the minds of probable, potential purchasers and buyers".

The market area analysis is presented in two sections, a regional analysis and general neighborhood analysis. The regional analysis will focus on a macro view of the subject market area and generally highlight current conditions that shape the nature of the regional economy. A more specific analysis that focuses on the subject's neighborhood area will follow.

## REGIONAL ANALYSIS

This section will discuss the greater economic trends (population, income, and employment) within the subject's region that may have an impact on the property now and in the foreseeable future. The following tables show the historic and projected growth rates for population, median household income, and unemployment within the region.

![img-4.jpeg](img-4.jpeg)

## Demographics

Oregon has experienced consistent population growth over the last decade. However, it has slowed down since 2020/2021 due to changes in the political climate and livability in the urban centers. As a result, residents have migrated to more rural/peripheral locations with more affordable real estate, which has been bolstered by more "work from home" jobs. However, positive increases, albeit slow, are starting to show signs of migration back into the city areas. The population is summarized in the table below.

## GENERAL NEIGHBORHOOD ANALYSIS

This section will discuss the general neighborhood and economic trends located in the primary market area that may have an impact on the property now and in the foreseeable future.

## Boundaries

The subject property is located in the city of Portland. The Portland area is bounded by the Columbia River/Vancouver Washington to the north, Gresham to the east, West Linn / Lake Oswego / Milwaukee to the south, Beaverton / Tigard to the west.

![img-7.jpeg](img-7.jpeg)

## General Neighborhood

The Greater Portland Oregon Central Business District (CBD), often referred to as Downtown Portland, serves as the city's primary commercial core and economic hub. It is characterized by a mix of high-rise office towers, government buildings, financial institutions, retail corridors, hotels, cultural venues, and transit-oriented development. The area features excellent multimodal access, including MAX light rail, streetcar lines, buses, and bike infrastructure, supporting high walkability scores and strong commuter flows. Real estate in the CBD includes a predominance of Class A and B office space, mixed-use buildings with ground-floor retail, and residential high-rises or condos in adaptive reuse projects. The market appeals to professionals seeking urban convenience, with proximity to landmarks such as Pioneer Courthouse Square, the Portland Art Museum, theaters, and the Willamette River waterfront. Property values benefit from institutional demand, though influenced by broader urban trends including office vacancy dynamics and residential densification.

## Immediate Area Overview

The subject property is located in Uptown Portland within the broader Northwest District (commonly referred to as Nob Hill or the Alphabet District). This area lies just northwest of the CBD and adjacent to the Pearl District, placing it within a desirable transition zone between the intensity of downtown and established residential neighborhoods. The district's commercial core, centered on NW 21st and NW 23rd Avenues, are characterized by pedestrian-oriented retail, restaurants, and services, supported by a mix of historic homes and early- to mid-century multifamily buildings. The nearby Pearl District contributes additional appeal through modern residential development, office uses, cultural amenities, and green spaces. The location offers convenient access to the CBD, employment centers, and transit.

126JG-111

16

---

## Page 20

MARKET AREA ANALYSIS (continued)

The immediate vicinity along West Burnside Street, between the I-405 Freeway and NW 23rd Avenue, includes multifamily residential properties, local commercial uses, Providence Park, and strong transit connectivity. The area is generally walkable, with a blend of commercial activity and quieter residential pockets.

In recent years, the area has experienced challenges related to the pandemic, civil unrest, and increases in transient criminal activity and homelessness. Local business groups have reported concerns regarding crime, loitering, drug activity, and reduced foot traffic, and several national and local retailers along West Burnside have closed. Police data from 2024–2025 indicate elevated offense levels in nearby Goose Hollow. Although the City has expanded shelter capacity, street-level impacts remain visible. Conditions tend to improve farther west into Nob Hill and south toward PSU, where residential density and pedestrian activity are more stable.

These factors have influenced real estate performance. Office uses have been most affected, with retail also impacted by reduced daytime workforce activity. Apartment properties have generally re-stabilized, though with slower rent growth and modestly higher turnover. Retail vacancy remains evident along West Burnside, including several long-term vacancies surrounding the subject property.

## Access and Linkages

The site allows good local and regional linkages to housing, employment, and services. The immediate subject is located along W. Burnside Street. The area is linked locally by W. Burnside Street, NW 21st &amp; 23rd Avenues, with the I-405 Freeway located a short distance to the east.

## Development

Over the past decade, development has been vigorous within the CBD. However, although it may appear that development is still ongoing, there are projects still underway that were slowed by the over-supply, coupled with significant office vacancies stemming from the work from home policies during the pandemic. The larger developments are typically high-rise with commercial retail on the ground floor and office / multi-family / hotel uses above. Most current development consists of replacement or remodeling of existing structures.

126JG-111

17

---

## Page 21

MARKET AREA ANALYSIS (continued)

## OUTLOOK AND CONCLUSION

Neighborhoods are constantly changing and continually cycle through four phases. The four phases include growth, stability, decline, and renewal. These phases are used to evaluate the changes that occur within a neighborhood over time. During the growth phase, the neighborhood is in the beginning of development and expansion. Following the growth phase is stability. The neighborhood may still grow, but at a much slower pace. A neighborhood enters a state of decline when it can no longer compete with adjacent neighborhoods. Lastly, renewal is the period during which a neighborhood is "rediscovered" because of affordability and convenience. The subject area has been in the growth/renewal phase as a long standing commercial and residential area locally, but showing signs of decline as businesses leaving the downtown core is still ongoing.

As the population and employment rates keep trending slowly upward, the subject market area is anticipated to continue improving, with appreciating property values in the long term. The adjacent table outlines some of the crucial neighborhood components that both institutional and private investors consider.

## Conclusion

Overall, there are no other adverse conditions that were evident that would have a detrimental impact on the subject property or the neighborhood. The long-term outlook for this area is average (improving), with stable to improving property values predicted to coincide with the neighborhood and region's economic growth.

## Page 45

HIGHEST AND BEST USE ANALYSIS

The Appraisal Institute defines the concept of highest and best use as follows:

"The reasonably probable and legal use of vacant land or an improved property, which is physically possible, appropriately supported, financially feasible, and results in the highest value. The four criteria the highest and best use must meet are legal permissibility, physical possibility, financial feasibility, and maximum profitability." 4

The highest and best use concept is based on traditional appraisal theory and reflects the attitudes of typical buyers and sellers who recognize that value is predicated on anticipation of future benefits. This theory is based on the wealth maximization of the owner. In the appraisal process, highest and best use concept typically reflects most probable use, and often relates to the most probable value of the property.

# AS VACANT

The subject is zoned CXd (Central Commercial w/ design overlay), city of Portland Planning Department. The Central Commercial (CXd) zone is intended to provide for commercial and mixed use development within Portland's most urban and intense areas, specifically the Central City and the Gateway Regional Center. A broad range of uses are allowed to reflect Portland's role as a commercial, cultural, residential, and governmental center. Development is intended to be very intense with high building coverage, large buildings, and buildings placed close together. Development is intended to be pedestrian-oriented with a strong emphasis on a safe and attractive streetscape. The Design overlay zone is applied to this zone. This zone allows a mix of commercial and residential uses (limitations set at 15,000 SF for industrial warehouse uses.

![img-38.jpeg](img-38.jpeg)

Legal / Physical: Basic Zoning Requirements; Minimum Density – None. Maximum Density – 4 to 1 FAR. Building Height – 75'. Site Coverage – 100% maximum, with none for landscaping. Overall, there is the potential for additional size/height with bonus provisions. Considering it location along a major arterial, a retail component would most likely, however, based on the current market, despite the temporarily high apartment vacancy, apartments are more likely. The subject is physically suitable for development, with functional / marketable land area and topography. In addition, the subject parcel is physically functional, with good exposure and linkages, and would have average appeal for development. With a site size of 0.23 acres (10,000 SF) a maximum development up to a total size of 40,000 SF (without bonus provisions) would be allowed.

Feasibility: Apartment uses in the subject's market area consist of older and newer (New to 100+ years) commercial buildings in this historic area, with average finishes and amenities. The rent levels are stable with vacancy rate used by brokers and owners at 5%, which includes credit loss.

126JG-111

16

Commercial uses in the subject's market area consist of older and newer (New to 100+ years) commercial buildings in this historic area, with average finishes and amenities. ## As-Vacant Conclusion

Based on the surrounding developments, current rental market trends noting some positive traits despite the continued civil unrest in portions of the downtown core, the highest and best use for the subject as vacant would be for apartment uses (based on slower demand for commercial uses).

## AS IMPROVED

The subject as a combined mixed-use development (49 apartment units and 4 retail suites totaling 45,704 SF GBA), the subject is a legal, but non-conforming use (above the maximum FAR). Considering the subject's age and location, this is not atypical. If a nonconforming use is discontinued for 3 continuous years, the nonconforming use rights are lost. When a structure containing a nonconforming use is damaged by fire or other causes beyond the control of the owner, the re-establishment of the nonconforming use is prohibited if the repair cost of the structure is more than 75 percent of its assessed value. The marketing activity experienced by developments in the subject's general market area demonstrates market acceptance of properties with features and physical characteristics similar to the subject. The subject is an average quality mixed-use project with average utility and local significance. It offers typical unit amenities with a functional and efficient design. The neighborhood characteristics have been previously described, and are also supportive of the current use.

Broader Vacancy in the Immediate Area: This area sits in a transitional urban zone with older/dated retail buildings (many from the mid-20th century or earlier), mixed-use with upper-floor apartments/offices, and high foot/vehicle traffic from Burnside's role as a major corridor. While the Nob Hill/Alphabet District to the west remains somewhat vibrant overall (with lower vacancy in prime stretches), the closer-in Goose Hollow/W Burnside blocks near 19th have pockets of softness, with some recent notable retail closures of Chipotle, Starbucks (including a former bank and several smaller tenants), and Hunnymilk within a 2 block span.

Older storefront business fluctuates, with some longer term tenants remaining, but vacant space being on the market for significant periods. In addition, the Costar survey doesn't account for retail spaces not on the market (shuttered for high renovation costs and other external issues previously noted, artificially suggesting retail is more stabilized. Smaller bays (e.g., 800–2,000 SF) in dated buildings along 1900–2000 blocks often cycle through tenants, with some sitting empty longer amid broader retail caution.

Metro retail vacancy is 4.5–5%, but urban close-in submarkets like this have higher effective availability due to sublease space, short-term/pop-up use, and tenant churn. Negative absorption continues in some Westside pockets, exacerbated by hybrid work reducing weekday foot traffic (Goose Hollow ties into downtown/office corridors), security/safety perceptions in transitional areas (as noted in nearby closures) and economic caution leading to slower leasing for dated spaces needing significant TI's.

## Page 48

HIGHEST AND BEST USE ANALYSIS (continued)

Despite this, the greater area isn't in full distress—strong residential density (apartments above retail), proximity to PSU/Goose Hollow, and Burnside's transit/ flow support rebound potential. Many vacancies are short-term or awaiting the right tenant (e.g., food/beverage, wellness, or indie retail that fits the character buildings). This narrows the overall pool of tenants, typically needing lower cost options as small business owners. Considering the current vacancy rate levels, there is a slowed demand for retail, especially for seasoned developments along W. Burnside.

The subject's occupied retail suites are slightly higher than the survey. However, these are longer term tenants, one a local coffee shop and the other an older long term tavern, popular during soccer season. The vacant spaces are less desirable currently, with deep bays and dated finishes. Therefore, a forecast slightly below the current survey average and well below the current occupied average is reasonable.

## Miscellaneous

**Market Participant Interviews:** The brokers involved in the sales transactions indicated that there is below average demand for properties in the immediate area. This is supported by the extended marketing period of the subject (over 2-years and a price reduction over the years of 27.53%). This is directly matched with loss of retail due to the influx of criminal elements and homelessness in the micro area along W. Burnside Street. Further decreases appear to be necessary to sell.

**Listings:** Limited comparable listings were discovered in researching this property. Most listings that were found were the extended market area, and were not comparable and did not lend credible information to this analysis. Extensive efforts were expended in searching for listings of similar character and appeal to the subject.

**Most Probable Buyer/Seller:** The most probable buyer is a private investor (considering the prices and development size). This is also similar for the typical seller in the market area.

## Conclusion: As Improved

Based on the analysis, the highest and best of the subject property, as improved, is the current mixed-use (multifamily and commercial). The current use is a legally sanctioned use that contributes value to the surrounding community, generally conforms to area development goals, and represents a productive, legal use of the subject property until renovations are supported by occupancy trends and lease rates.

## Page 49

VALUATION

# INTRODUCTION

The social, economic, governmental and environmental forces influencing the subject property have been presented. The specific physical and location characteristics of the site and improvements have been described. The highest and best use of the subject property as improved is concluded to be the existing use. At the request of the client, the purpose of this appraisal report is to the appraisal assignment is to develop and report our opinion of the As-Is Leased Fee Value. However, there are deferred maintenance/absorption/income loss deductions to be made, therefore, a Prospective Leased Fee Value at Stabilized Occupancy will be presented first. The market value(s) were estimated using all applicable steps of the appraisal process.

# VALUATION APPROACHES

The Cost Approach was not presented in this appraisal due to the subjectivity and difficulty in determining accrued depreciation for older buildings. A typical purchaser of the subject property would not consider this approach as a reliable indicator. Therefore, the exclusion of this valuation approach is reasonable and does not reduce the credibility of this appraisal.

The Income Capitalization Approach is the process of converting the anticipated income of a property to value by a process known as capitalization. This method converts a periodic series of future benefits (net income) into a present value by use of an appropriate, market derived, rate of return. The market capitalization rate reflects the specific income pattern, return on investment, and change in the value of the investment. The Income Capitalization Approach is considered to reflect common investor behavior, and is typically an accurate measure of the value of income-producing properties.

The Sales Comparison Approach is the value derived by comparing the subject property to similar properties that have sold recently, applying appropriate units of comparison, and making necessary adjustments to the sales prices. This approach may be used to value vacant land or improved properties. It is most reliable for special use and owner occupied properties that are not typically purchased based on income producing ability. This approach is used for the valuation of the improvements.

# VALUE CONCLUSION

The income and sales comparison approaches are performed and reconciled to develop our opinion of the Prospective Leased Fee Value at Stabilized Occupancy, with adjustments made to reconcile the As-Is Leased Fee Value.

## COMPARABLE LEASE ANALYSIS – RETAIL SUITES

The following analysis generally ranks the subject among the comparables using tangible percentage adjustments to illustrate the relative comparability to the subject. The comparables are adjusted to the subject property consistent with standard valuation (appraisal) methodology. A positive percentage adjustment indicates that a comparable is inferior to the subject regarding a specific (or set of related) characteristics) and is therefore adjusted upward to simulate rough parity with the subject property. Conversely, negative percentage adjustment indicates that a comparable is superior to the subject in some regard and so is adjusted downward. ## Page 57

Floor Area – The floor area adjustment is made to reflect the relationship between size and unit values. Typically, the larger the property the somewhat lower the unit value (economy of scale). The comparables were adjusted 0.0% to -10.0% to reflect the differing sizes (risk).

Quality and Utility – The quality and utility adjustment is intended to treat any differences related to the function and use of the property that would be considered significantly divergent from the subject spaces for general retail use. The comparables was adjusted between 0.0% and 20.0% for their general quality and utility with regard to appeal, and use.

Parking – The parking adjustment accounts for onsite parking allowances. These adjustments take into consideration other parking options in front of the subject. The comparables were adjusted between 0.0% and -5.0% for their off-street parking. The street parking is not metered surrounding the subject, which places lower value on off-street parking.

Market Condition – This adjustment is intended to reflect intangible factors other than time that affect marketability such as the availability of financing, atypical market events, and the demand for the property type. Adjustments were not needed as none of the comparables suffered or benefited from any atypical market conditions.

## RETAIL SUITES MARKET RATE CONCLUSIONS

Based on the preceding analysis the comparables support a market rent for the subject property between $18.60/SF and $25.00/SF as adjusted. For the vacant units, and any turnover, $20.00/SF is reasonable. However, given the subjects occupied units being of average condition and strong history, the urban-vintage buildouts, appeal, moderate sizes, and overall niche appeal near the stadium, a rate within the upper-middle portion of this range is supported. Overall, with the occupied rates combined with the vacant rates averages at $22.29/SF, reflecting a rate still with the upper-middle portion of the range. Overall, an average rate of $23.00/SF/NNN/annually is concluded for the subject's storefront retail spaces.

## COMPARABLE RENT ANALYSIS: APARTMENTS

The following analysis generally ranks the subject among the comparables using tangible dollar/percentage adjustments to illustrate the relative comparability to the subject. The comparables are adjusted to the subject property consistent with standard valuation (appraisal) methodology. A positive percentage adjustment indicates that a comparable is inferior to the subject regarding a specific (or set of related) characteristics) and is therefore adjusted upward to simulate rough parity with the subject property. Conversely, negative dollar/percentage adjustment-indicates-that a comparable is superior to the subject in some regard and so is adjusted downward. The resulting range of adjusted indicators provides a quantifiable illustration of the subjective-logic-upon-which the comparable analysis is based. The conclusion-is further refined in the narrative analysis that follows.

The following comparable rent exhibits are unadjusted, with the full adjustment grid(s) being located in the addenda for review. Following these exhibits is a summary of the rent adjustment analysis and forecast conclusions of market rent for the subject.

## Comparable Rent Analysis Summary &amp; Conclusions

0-Bedroom 1.0-Bath (studio) Analysis: In terms of forecasting rents, despite the current market with slowly increasing rents and decreasing inflation (after a moderate period of higher inflation), increasing rents significantly ads little validity of the conclusions and is fairly speculative (considering the rent controls and fines owed if rents increased higher than the State/Local ordinances). However, this should partly be offset by the higher interest rates and rising house prices, making it harder for people to afford buying a home, giving continued strength to the rental market. The comparables ranged from $804 to $969 per month once adjusted. The subject rents are $960 &amp; $985 per month. The lowest (most recent) was signed in June 2025, with the highest rent signed in 2024, but located on the 3rd floor (superior).

## Rent Increase Analysis

The following rent increase analysis will be presented to address any potential rent increases and conclude individual rents for each unit at the subject. Due to rent control limitations/rules, forecasting an average rent is problematic as each unit is subject to differing dates and requirements. Therefore, individual rents are forecast specifically for each unit, with the sum of all rents for the monthly forecast concluded. This monthly forecast is then used to calculate the annual forecast rent and the resulting average unit rent (rounded).

To determine if rent increases would even be allowed, the move-in dates, lease expiration dates and date/amount of the last increase were analyzed. This information provided did not suggest a significant deficiency in rent amounts as market rents and market rent increases have been achieved on a regular basis, with no forecast changes as the subject's current rents are generally at market, with some recent rent increases.

This adjustment table is located on the following page, with our final rent conclusions per unit are presented thereafter. The following table analyses each unit and if an increase is warranted and/or applied. Despite the current market conditions, re-rented units do not pose any problems in the market (within the subject's market tier), with owner's achieving market rents.

This equates to an annual Potential Gross Income of $654,082. The forecast is within market standards and reasonable for the subject's units.

## VACANCY AND CREDIT LOSS

Apartments are typically trading using a vacancy and credit loss allocation of 5% of PGI for stabilized properties over the life of the property. A stabilized rate of 5% (over a typical holding period) is being used by investors and knowledgeable market participants. Therefore, there is no compelling reason that was found to depart from market practice. For the retail suites, the subjects location within the micro-market along W. Burnside Street, less desirable utility of the units (deep and narrow) and the competition from superior product, the subjects units will continue to see higher vacancy as a result. as previously indicated in the Highest and Best Use section, retail vacancy, especially for the immediate area, a stabilized rate of 7.5% (over a typical holding period) is being used by investors and knowledgeable market participants.

## EFFECTIVE GROSS INCOME

Based on the concluded potential gross income and vacancy and credit loss, effective gross income (EGI) for the subject property is forecast at $800,188. The forecast is 15.71% higher than the 2025 collected income ($674,460). This increase stems from forecasted income for the vacant retail spaces, apartment rent increases and inclusion of two currently downed apartment units. Therefore, income loss/deferred maintenance (absorption) will be addressed when reconciling the subjects As-Is Leased Fee Value.

## OPERATING EXPENSES

The 2025 and 9-months of 2024 were provided. Additional years were not available. Expense comparables for this type of property of very subjective and would add very little credibility to this analysis. Therefore, actuals are given the most consideration, but forecast with some typical expense standards not typically reported by smaller developments, but are real costs, nevertheless. Overall, the subjects final conclusions are presented in the following analysis.

## OVERALL CAPITALIZATION RATE ANALYSIS

The following analysis generally ranks the subject among the comparables using tangible percentage adjustments to illustrate the relative comparability to the subject. The comparables are adjusted to the subject property consistent with standard valuation (appraisal) methodology. A positive percentage adjustment indicates that a comparable is inferior to the subject regarding a specific (or set of related) characteristics) and is therefore adjusted upward to simulate rough parity with the subject property. Conversely, negative percentage adjustment-indicates-that a comparable is superior to the subject in some regard and so is adjusted downward. The resulting range of adjusted indicators provides a quantifiable illustration of the subjective logic upon which the comparable analysis is based. The conclusion-is further refined in the narrative analysis that follows.

Market Extraction Analysis: Overall capitalization rates are market-extracted by dividing the net operating income of a sold property by its cash-equivalent sale price. The primary factors influencing capitalization rates are: 1) The quality and certainty ('durability') of the net operating income stream; 2) risk factors; 3) physical and location characteristics of the property; 4) the competitive market position of the property; and 5) the potential for future appreciation. Capitalization rate comparables are summarized on the following table (next page). The unadjusted comparable sales used in this analysis have a moderate range between 5.19% and 7.60%. The following presents the analysis outlining the individual adjustments made to arrive at an adjusted rate range for the subject and a concluded capitalization rate.

## Page 72

INCOME CAPITALIZATION APPROACH (continued)

Income Stability: (Location/Rent Levels) – This adjustment is intended to account for differences in the types and/or quality of income streams based on locational attributes and rent levels. For instance, a comparable located in an area of higher demand/superior income levels, poses lower risk for turnover and rent drops in a recessionary market. Adjustments between -7.5% and 10.0% were made.

Building Class: (Effects on Investor Expectations) – This adjustment is intended to account for differences in income potential as it pertains to its quality and/or condition. For instance, high quality and well maintained properties tend to maintain market rents as opposed to lower quality developments (including the potential for higher maintenance, effecting the net operating income). Adjustments between -7.5% and 15.0% were made.

Development Size: (Liquidity/Economies of Scale) – This adjustment is intended to account for differences in development sizes and their liquidity potential. For instance, all else being equal, a smaller development typically has higher demand (financing options/larger investor pool). Therefore, adjustments between 30.0% and -10.0% were made.

Market Condition (Financing / Market Events/Demand) – This adjustment is intended to reflect intangible factors that affect marketability such as the availability of financing, atypical market events, and the demand for the property type. So far, according to several brokers interviewed, prices haven't significantly dropped because of those factors, but time will tell how long the market will shrug off these conditions. Overall, no adjustments were made.

Summary: The adjusted comparables range between 7.27% and 7.81%. The lower portion of the range is not typical and generally reflective of all cash buyers, which are able to purchase the investments at a lower capitalization rate (no lending restraints). This is a good indicator of investor anticipations of the market, but not reflective of a typical buyer needing loans considering the price points of mixed-use (apartments over commercial) in the market area. Although capitalizations rates have been ticking upwards (even for cash buyers to some extent) as a reflection of the current market, this is anticipated to stabilize over the next 12 months.

## Direct Capitalization Rate Conclusion

Considering the narrow range, it is not unreasonable to conclude a rate within the upper-middle portion of the range or 7.75% for the subject property considering the price per SF, moderate rent levels and expectations from market area participants. The income table is located on the following page followed by the value conclusion.

SALES COMPARISON APPROACH

The Sales Comparison Approach develops an indication of value by comparing the subject property with similar recently sold properties. The most relevant unit of comparison is the sales price per square foot. The comparable sales analyzed were confirmed by the listing and/or buyer's agent.

## COMPARABLE SALES ANALYSIS

The following analysis generally ranks the subject among the comparables using tangible percentage adjustments to illustrate the relative comparability to the subject. The comparables are adjusted to the subject property consistent with standard valuation (appraisal) methodology. A positive percentage adjustment indicates that a comparable is inferior to the subject regarding a specific (or set of related) characteristics) and is therefore adjusted upward to simulate rough parity with the subject property. Conversely, negative percentage adjustment indicates that a comparable is superior to the subject-in-some regard and so is adjusted downward. The resulting range of adjusted indicators provides a quantifiable illustration of the subjective logic upon which the comparable analysis is based. The conclusion is further refined in the narrative analysis that follows.

## Adjustment Analysis

The unadjusted comparable sales used in this analysis range between $134.01/SF and $248.25/SF. The following presents the analysis outlining the individual adjustments made to arrive at an adjusted price/unit range for the subject and a concluded price/SF. A time adjustment of 3% were made to address the market conditions as the times of sale.

**Economic Location** – The economic location adjustment is intended to account for differences in the immediate market areas of the comparables relative to the subject (size, economic differences, access, and overall appeal). Adjustments were made between -5.0% and -10.0%.

**Floor Area** – The size adjustment is intended to treat any differences related to the size of the development, which greatly impacts the price, which impacts the availability of investors. Adjustments of -5.0% were made to all of the comparables.

**Condition / Quality / Utility** – The condition, quality and utility adjustment is intended to treat any differences related to the condition/quality as how this impacts its marketability. As for utility, function and use of the property that would be considered significantly divergent from the subject as a mixed use building and would in some way impact the income. Adjustments between 10.0% and -40.0% were made.

**Access / Exposure** – The access and exposure adjustment is intended to treat any differences related to its ability to attract tenants in terms of its specific locational attributes not covered in the economic location adjustment. Adjustments of 5.0% were made to all of the comparables.

**Parking** – The parking adjustment accounts for onsite parking allowances. These adjustments take into consideration other parking options in front of the subject. No adjustments were made.

|  Contingency | 1% | $2,246 | $1,965 | $1,683 | $1,401 | $1,120 | $838 | $557 | $275  |
|  Total Absorption Costs |  | $226,868 | $198,427 | $169,985 | $141,544 | $113,102 | $84,661 | $56,219 | $27,778  |
|  Discounted to Present Value @ | 8% | $954,354 |  |  |  |  |  |  |   |
|  Concluded Absorption Cost |  |  |  |  |  | $950,000 |  |  |   |

Although the previously concluded lease rate was $23.00/SF, this was reflective of an average of currently leased and vacant suites. The vacant suites were forecast at $20.00/SF; therefore, this rate will be used when forecasting the income loss. In addition, the vacant suites are allocated $25.00/SF for TI's to cure the units deferred maintenance to reflect the forecast rate. Based on current market conditions previously analyzed, and discussions with local market participants, an absorption period of 8 quarters (2 years) is reasonable. With inclusions of developers profit, leasing commissions, contingencies, and discounted at 8%, brings the total loss for the retail component to $950,000 rounded. Overall, this is a reasonable conclusion for the subject and well supported by the market and a reflection of investor behaviors.

## Absorption/Rent Increase Loss/Deferred Maintenance Analysis Conclusion

By combining the apartment and retail components losses ($40,000 + $950,000) bring the total absorption costs to $990,000. This is a reasonable conclusion considering the risk and costs involved for bringing the subject to a reasonable level of stabilization. This total will be deducted from the previously concluded stabilized value to reconcile the As-Is Leased Fee Value as presented on the following page.

|  Combined Absorption Cost Summary  |   |   |
| --- | --- | --- |
|  Retail Absorption | - | $950,000  |
|  Apartment Absorption | - | $40,000  |
|  Total Absorption Costs |  | $990,000  |

# COMPANY PROFILE

Jackson Group NW, Inc. is a commercial real estate appraisal and consulting firm located in Portland, Oregon. The goal of the company is to provide appraisal and consultation services in a professional and timely manner. We provide appraisal services for properties located in Oregon and Washington. We are dedicated to the company's goal of providing quality appraisal and consultation services on a personal basis.

We have extensive experience in appraising and providing consultation services. Our scope of experience includes multiple property types and various ownership interests from small owner occupied properties to large institutional grade investments.

W. Paul Jackson, MAI has worked as a commercial appraiser and consultant since 1992. Mr. Jackson has experience in appraising multiple property types. He also has experience in appraising property for estate planning and court testimony. Over the years, in addition to the standard property types, he has developed valuable experience and expertise in the valuation of Hotels/Motels, gas stations/c-stores, mobile home parks, golf courses, institutional grade government buildings, complex residential, agricultural, and a handful of other unique property types. Mr. Jackson prides himself on his ability to provide quality professional appraisal services that are both reliable and on time. Whatever your appraisals need, be it a simple owner-occupied office or a complex re-development, you can rely on Mr. Jackson to provide you with the service you need.

Joseph B. Skilton has worked in real estate since 1978 as a land survey fieldman and drafter, cartographer (Ticor), real estate research analyst, corporate librarian, computer analyst, and real estate publisher (Real Estate Transaction Journal). Mr. Skilton joined us in 1999 and specializes in retail, general commercial, and special-use property appraisals.

Joseph A. Swaney had a prior career working in the information technology field as a computer programmer and database analyst. His first introduction to real estate was as a surveyor's chainman in his teenage years. He joined Jackson Group NW, Inc. in February 2005 as an assistant to W. Paul Jackson, and became a Certified General Appraiser in 2012.

William E. Leavens has worked in the real estate community since high school where he did general construction, property management and accounts receivable for Leavens Investments, a family-owned rental property business. He began appraising in January 2003, specializing in apartment appraisals. Over the past 20+ years, Will has gained extensive experience in appraising and providing consultation services for multifamily/mixed-use developments as a high value senior employee and also as the owner of his own appraisal firm. Will brings a high level of customer service and expertise to our firm that is of great value to our clients and us.

Garth W. Bergeson has worked in commercial real estate appraisal since June 2004 and is currently certified in both Oregon and Washington. Garth has a broad base of experience ranging from standard commercial real estate appraisal work to complex land valuations. Garth brings a level of expert analysis and critical thinking to our team that is reflected in his clear and logical writing style.

JACKSONGROUPNW

# COMPANY PROFILE (CONTINUED)

To better serve our clients, Jackson Group NW, Inc. maintains state of the art computer equipment, real estate analytical software programs, libraries of comparable and resource data, property sales, lease transactions, property data computer retrieval systems, and other necessary tools and information relevant to the real estate appraisal and consultation field.

Jackson Group NW, Inc. considers its clients to be its most valuable assets. We have served various small and large financial institutions, corporations, developers, private individuals, attorneys, accountants, and government agencies. Our appraisal experience extends to all types of commercial, industrial, residential, apartment, resort, agricultural, and special use properties.

Past appraisal assignments have been conducted to serve various functions, including mortgage financing, property purchase and disposition, lease/rent arbitration, litigation support, condemnation, property tax analysis and appeal services, and corporate planning purposes.

# PROFESSIONAL SERVICES

- Valuation Reports: The market valuation of real property interests (fee simple, leasehold, leased fee, etc.) in various types of properties is the primary focus of most real property appraisal assignments performed by Jackson Group NW, Inc.
- Real Estate Consulting: Jackson Group NW, Inc. also conducts consulting assignments relating to all property types. Providing competent, unbiased, professional guidance on diversified problems in real estate has taken on ever increasing importance as a method of reducing the risks associated with the real estate field. The firm has undertaken such tasks as discounted cash flow analysis, highest and best use analysis, market studies, subdivision analysis, and market / feasibility analysis.
- Arbitration, Litigation Support, and Expert Witness Services: Jackson Group NW, Inc. also offers services for real estate matters involving arbitration (ground rent renegotiations, tenant space lease renegotiations, etc.). We also offer litigation support services to attorneys with various real estate problems. It is our goal to provide not only offensive, but defensive strategies, in a fair and unbiased manner, in order to expose the strengths and weaknesses of a particular case. Finally, we are experienced in working with attorneys in preparation for expert witness testimony.
- Appraisal Review Services: An appraisal review serves the purpose of analyzing the content and conclusions of an appraisal report. Clients consider an appraisal review to be a valuable tool in determining the credibility of an appraisal report, in order to reduce the risks associated with important lending, legal, and investment decisions.
- Property Tax Analysis and Appeal Services: The real property tax liability for property has a direct impact on its operating/holding expenses, net income, and associated market value. Therefore, competent representation before government agencies regarding ad valorem taxes can be critical in keeping this expense item in check.

a. Purpose of the Assignment
The purpose of the assignment is to develop and report an informed and independent opinion of the Market Value of the Subject Property.

The report you prepare is intended to be used by the Financial Institution as an aid in underwriting a loan, loan modification or loan extension; classification or monitoring of a loan; and/or the disposition or monitoring of REO or loan collateral, which may constitute a federally-related real estate transaction for purposes of applicable federal appraisal regulations. The Financial Institution may, without your prior authorization or a notice to you, provide your report to other parties for their use, including without limitation in lending-related activities.

c. Acceptance of the Assignment
You must accept or decline this assignment within one (1) business day.

d. Web Portal
The Financial Institution (Client) has engaged MountainSeed Appraisal Management, LLC (MountainSeed) to provide certain appraisal management company services to the Financial Institution. You understand that the Financial Institution reserves the right to withhold payment in the event the assignment is reassigned due to your failure to meet timing and delivery requirements.

e. Requirement to Decline the Assignment or Stop Work

You must decline this assignment if you are not geographically competent, the assignment falls outside of your scope of practice restrictions, or you know of any reason you would not be able to provide an unbiased and independent professional judgment as to the Market Value of the Subject Property. Furthermore, you must stop work and immediately notify MountainSeed if you later determine that you are not qualified to complete the assignment. Set" and add the date and time of the appointment in the appropriate section of the order.

iii. Uniform Standard

You and your work product must comply with the regulations, guidelines, and standards specified in the MountainSeed General Guidelines for Financial Institutions, Commercial Appraisal Services (herein referred to as the Additional Requirements). While you must comply, and must ensure that your work product complies, with those provisions in the respective federal laws and regulations that are applicable to you and your performance of this assignment, this Engagement Letter does not require you to guaranty compliance with portions of those federal laws and regulations that do not apply to your performance of this assignment or over which you have no control whatsoever. However, you must be knowledgeable about and comply with any provisions of the laws, regulations, and guidelines discussed above that apply to your work. v. Nondiscrimination

The Fair Housing Act, Equal Credit Opportunity Act, and other federal, state, and local laws and regulations broadly prohibit discrimination in real estate appraisal and lending services based on legally protected characteristics. These protected characteristics, depending on the applicable law or regulation, may include race, color, religion, sex, sexual orientation, gender identity, age, marital status, disability, familial status, exercise of any federally protected civil right, receipt of income derived from any public assistance program, birthplaces of residents of the property or in the neighborhood, national origin of the prospective owners or occupants of the subject property or of the present owners or occupants of the properties in the vicinity of the subject property.

In accepting this assignment, you acknowledge the importance of these protections against discrimination and agree that your performance of the assignment, including without limitation your development and reporting of the appraisal and your interactions with all persons, will not be based in part or in whole on information involving any legally protected characteristics, regardless of your intention, unless expressly permitted by law. You agree that you and persons acting under your direction or control are knowledgeable of and will abide by all applicable federal, state, and local laws and regulations governing nondiscrimination.

Furthermore, in conformity with USPAP, if no law or regulation prohibits the use of or reliance upon a protected characteristic, then the use of or reliance upon that characteristic is permitted only to the extent that it is both essential to the assignment and necessary for credible assignment results. See Exceptions.

1. Specifically Prohibited/Unacceptable Practices

To avoid discrimination, or a suggestion of discrimination, by you or by other parties who may utilize or rely on your appraisal, you agree that you will not:

a) Act with bias or otherwise discriminate against or treat differently any individual or group

---

## Page 102

based on a protected characteristic.

b) Consider, discuss, or refer to any protected characteristic of any party or with regard to the neighborhood or community within which the subject property is located in your appraisal development and/or report or in communications with other parties, including MountainSeed, unless expressly permitted by law or regulation.

c) Use, rely on, consider, or discuss in any analysis, opinion or conclusion, any assumptions, stereotypes, or generalizations related to protected characteristics (or nonprotected characteristics intentionally used as a substitute for a protected characteristic).

d) Employ terminology or veiled language as code words that may signal underlying bias, including but not limited to desirable location/neighborhood, diverse, gentrified, pride of ownership, predominantly White or Black or Hispanic neighborhood, services or amenities geared toward a protected class or using generational descriptors such as Gen X, Millennial, or Boomer.

These specific unacceptable practices do not limit your responsibility to comply with all other discrimination prohibitions in the applicable laws and regulations.

## 2. Exceptions

a) In limited circumstances, applicable nondiscrimination laws and regulations may permit the consideration of protected characteristics in the performance of an appraisal (e.g., the prohibition against discrimination based on family may not be applicable to an appraisal of housing legally designated for older persons). If a protected characteristic is considered under such a limited exception, that such consideration is required or expressly permitted by law or regulation, one must conspicuously state in any communication, report, or analysis (1) the protected characteristic(s) considered, (2) the law or regulation that requires or expressly permits consideration/discussion, and (3) demonstrate that this use is essential to the assignment and necessary for credible assignment results.

b) If no law or regulation prohibits the use of or reliance upon a protected characteristic, then one must demonstrate that the use of or reliance upon that characteristic is both essential to the assignment and necessary for credible assignment results.

## 3. Reporting of Concerns and Accusations

In the event that any party expresses a concern or makes any accusation regarding discrimination, you agree that you will immediately notify MountainSeed and cooperate with MountainSeed in its investigation and resolution of the matter.

vi. State-Specific Requirements

Appraisals are expected to comply with any state-specific requirements regarding the disclosure of: (a) MountainSeed's AMC registration or license number, as applicable; and/or (b) the fee paid for your services. Pursuant to state law, MountainSeed cannot prohibit the appraiser from disclosing the appraisal fee within the appraisal report. Furthermore, MountainSeed does not require the appraiser to disclose the AMC registration/license number or appraisal fee within the appraisal report, however in the following states, you should disclose the AMC registration and/or your fee within the body of the appraisal report. (Note: This list is provided for informational purposes only. It is your responsibility to verify disclosure requirements with the licensing board in the state where the subject property is located.)

|  State | Appraiser to Disclose in Report (AMC #, Fee, or Both) | Special Instructions  |

|  Alabama | AMC # | None  |

2. Assignments Involving Illinois Property

For appraisal assignments involving property located in the state of Illinois, you must comply with the requirements of Illinois law and the Illinois Administrative Code. Illinois has established state law governing appraisal assignments in which an AMC is involved. You must comply with Illinois law, including the provisions of Illinois Administrative Code Section 1455.250.

a. Client(s) &amp; Intended User(s)

MountainSeed is an appraisal management company (AMC) and is engaging you on the

---

## Page 104

Financial Institutions behalf, acting as the Financial Institutions agent. As the Financial Institution is engaging you through MountainSeed (an agent), the Financial Institution is also a client. Accordingly, notwithstanding any provision to the contrary contained in this Engagement Letter, you must identify MountainSeed as the client and the Financial Institution as the intended user in the appraisal report.

## b. AMC Illinois Registration Number

The appraiser shall prominently display the appraisal management company's Illinois registration number (see top of this engagement letter) and expiration date as follows: "The appraisal management company's Illinois registration number is 558.xxxxxx and it expires on (month/day/year)."

Verify current license number and expiration date at: https://online-dfpr.micropact.com/Lookup/LicenseLookup.aspx

## c. Fee Payment Statement

Per Illinois Administrative Code Section 1455.250, the appraiser shall prominently display the appropriate statement as follows:

1) An independent appraiser by employment or contract for a specific assignment, the appraiser shall prominently display the appraisal fee received from the appraisal management company in the certification as follows: "The compensation for this appraisal assignment is $____."

2) An employee appraiser for a specific assignment, the appraiser shall prominently display the appraisal fee received from the appraisal management company in the certification as follows: "The compensation for this appraisal assignment is $____."

3) An employee appraiser who receives a salary and does not receive a fee for the assignment, the employee appraiser shall prominently display the following language: "The appraiser is a salaried employee and received no appraisal fee for the assignment."

4) An appraiser for a specific assignment, the appraiser shall prominently display the appraisal management company's Illinois registration number and expiration date as follows: "The appraisal management company's Illinois registration number is _______ and it expires on (month/day/year)."

d. To our knowledge, the owner of the property under appraisement has not been advised that interior images may be required under the assignment criteria. It is advised that upon contact with the owner, you notify them of any interior images that may be required.

## vii. Form-Specific Requirements

### 1. FHLMC Form 71A

If Form 71A is used, the appraiser must conspicuously state in an addendum that the pre-printed definition of market value, contingent &amp; limiting conditions, certification, and FHLMC Form 461 (lease analysis) referenced on Form 71A are outdated and are not incorporated in the report. (See 71A Addendum Example below).

At a minimum, the report must contain a current definition of market value, assumptions and limiting

---

## Page 105

conditions, and certifications that comply with the current USPAP.

## 71A Addendum Example:

FORM 71A CONTAINS THE FOLLOWING PRE-PRINTED ELEMENTS THAT ARE OUTDATED AND COMPROMISE COMPLIANCE WITH CURRENT STANDARDS AND GUIDELINES. THESE PRE-PRINTED ELEMENTS ARE NOT INCORPORATED IN THE REPORT.

1. Definition of Market Value Not current (c.1975) as printed on Form 71A and not consistent with the current FIRREA definition. (See FIRREA Title XI § 323.2 Definitions. (h) Market value)
2. Contingent &amp; Limiting Conditions Not current with USPAP as they contain assumptions that appear to require explicit identification as Extraordinary Assumptions (e.g. assumptions regarding condition). [See USPAP Standard 2-2 (a)(xiii)]
3. Certification Not current or consistent with the current minimum certification required by USPAP. (See USPAP Standard 2-3)
4. FHMLC Form 461 (Lease Analysis) Inadequate to meet current standards and requirements for development and reporting.

2. THE APPRAISER HAS REPLACED THESE WITH ADDENDA AND ATTACHMENTS THAT COMPLY WITH CURRENT STANDARDS AND GUIDELINES.

## 2. FHLMC Form 71B

If a 71B is ordered, the appraiser must conspicuously state in an addendum that FHLMC 239 (certification and statement of limiting conditions, including definition of market value) and FHLMC Form 461 (lease analysis) referenced on form 71B are outdated and are not incorporated in the report. (See 71B Addendum Example below)

At a minimum, the report must contain a current definition of market value, assumptions and limiting conditions, and certifications that comply with the current USPAP.

## 71B Addendum Example:

1. FORM 71B CONTAINS PRE-PRINTED REFERENCES TO THE FOLLOWING FORMS THAT ARE OUTDATED AND COMPROMISE COMPLIANCE WITH CURRENT STANDARDS AND GUIDELINES. THESE ELEMENTS ARE NOT INCORPORATED IN THE REPORT. FHLMC 239 Outdated due to:

1. Definition of Market Value) Not current (c.1975) and not consistent with the current FIRREA definition. (See FIRREA Title XI § 323.2 Definitions. (h) Market value)
2. Contingent &amp; Limiting Conditions Not current with USPAP as they contain assumptions that appear to require explicit identification as Extraordinary Assumptions (e.g. assumptions regarding condition). [See USPAP Standard 2-2 (a)(xiii)]
3. Certification Not current or consistent with the current minimum certification required by USPAP. (See USPAP Standard 2-3)

2. FHMLC Form 461 (Lease Analysis) Inadequate to meet current standards and requirements for development and reporting.

3. THE APPRAISER HAS REPLACED THESE WITH ADDENDA AND ATTACHMENTS THAT COMPLY WITH CURRENT STANDARDS AND GUIDELINES.
